in polynomial time. Moreover, it is easy to see that we can answer independence queries in polynomial time for any partition matroid, particularly for
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
. We can thus apply Theorem
4.1
on
f
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{B}}
with
\zeta=(1-1/e-1/2)

to find a solution
X\in\mathcal{I}
such that
5
5

5

We remark that we could select
\zeta
to be arbitrarily small and get a better guarantee in the statement of Lemma
4.14
. We have chosen this value to simplify the calculations as improving the guarantee of Lemma
4.14
does not improve the overall result.

\displaystyle g(\emptyset)-g(X)=f(X)\geq(1-1/e-\zeta)f(\hat{\text{OPT}})=(1-1/e-\zeta)(g(\emptyset)-g(\hat{\text{OPT}}))=\frac{1}{2}(g(\emptyset)-g(\hat{\text{OPT}}))\,,

where we used that
\hat{\text{OPT}}
is one feasible solution, i.e.,
\hat{\text{OPT}}\in\mathcal{I}
.
This in turn implies that

\frac{1}{2}\left(g(\hat{\text{OPT}})+g(\emptyset)\right)\geq g(X)\,.

Using the upper bounds on
g(\emptyset)
and
g(\hat{\text{OPT}})
of Lemma
4.15
we thus have found a set
X
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{O},\mu^{\prime}_{O})+\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{D},\mu^{\prime}_{D}))\geq g(X)\,.

Now using the last property of Lemma
4.15
we can output a solution
S^{*}
whose cost is at most

\displaystyle\frac{(1+2\varepsilon)}{2}\left(\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{O},\mu^{\prime}_{O})+\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{D},\mu^{\prime}_{D})\right)+O(\varepsilon\text{opt})\,.

as required.

Finally, each of the above steps runs in polynomial time: the algorithm of Theorem
4.1
is polynomial time, and the last property of Lemma
4.15
used to obtain
S^{*}
is polynomial-time.
Moreover, the number of guesses of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
is at most
n^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
, as argued after the description of that procedure. So we can, in polynomial time, try all possibilities and, among all solutions found (one for each guess of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
), return one that minimizes the cost and, in particular, has cost at most that of
S^{*}
(which was analyzed assuming the guess of
\mathcal{R}_{0}
was correct). We thus have a polynomial time algorithm that returns a solution that satisfies the guarantee of the lemma.
∎

4.6.1
Proof of Lemma
4.15

Proof of Property 1.

Given
X\subseteq F_{\mathcal{B}}
, we argue that we can evaluate
g(X)
in polynomial time. For a center
c\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
, define

\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)=\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)-\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)\,.

In other words, using that
d(p,c)\leq d(p,X)
for
p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}
if
C_{c}
is not hit by
X
,

\displaystyle\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{if $C_{c}$ is hit by $X$,}\\
\min_{c^{\prime}\in X\cup\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}}(d(p,c^{\prime})-d(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda))&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(24)

We remark that
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)\geq 0
by definition.
Let
D_{0}
denote the clients belonging to clusters
C_{c}
with
c\in\mathcal{R}_{0}
.
With this notation,
g(X)
is the minimum value

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)

(25)

over all subsets
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
with
|\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}|=|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
. We further have that the value of
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)
for
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
is independent of other centers in
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
. We can thus obtain the best choice of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
by evaluating
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)
for every
c\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
and choose the
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
ones of smallest value. Notice that the algorithm knows
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
since it equals
|S_{0}|-|\mathcal{Q}|-|\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}|-|\mathcal{R}_{0}|=|\mathcal{B}|-|\mathcal{Q}|-|\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}|-|\mathcal{R}_{0}|
. After we have obtained
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
in polynomial time, we can evaluate
g(X)
in polynomial time by simply calculating the sum.

Proof of Property 2.

In the proof of this property it will be convenient to use definition (
25
) of
g
.
We start by verifying that
f
is monotone. I.e., that
f(Y)\geq f(X)
when
X\subseteq Y
, which is equivalent to verifying that
g(Y)\leq g(X)
. Let
g(X)
equal

\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)

for some set
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
. Then
g(Y)
is at most

\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,Y\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)

As trivially
d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda)\leq d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)
,
d(p,Y\cup\Lambda)\leq d(p,X\cup\Lambda)
and
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\leq\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)
(see (
24
)), we have
g(Y)\leq g(X)
as required. Moreover, as
f(\emptyset)=0
by definition, non-negativity follows.

We proceed to verify that
f
is submodular, i.e., that for every
X\subseteq Y\subseteq F_{B}
and
c_{0}\in F_{B}-Y
,
f(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-f(X)\geq f(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-f(Y)
, or equivalently

\displaystyle g(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-g(X)\leq g(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-g(Y)\,.

(26)

Let
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{X},\mathcal{R}_{1}^{Y}
, and
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{Y+c_{0}}
be subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of cardinality
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
such that

\displaystyle g(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)

\displaystyle g(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,Y\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)

\displaystyle g(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})

We shall define
{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}
to be a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of cardinality
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
so that the upper bound

\displaystyle g(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\leq\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})

allows us to verify Inequality (
26
). First notice that no matter the definition of
{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}(d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)-d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}(d(p,(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)-d(p,X\cup\Lambda))

(27)

is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in D-D_{0}}(d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)-d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}(d(p,(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})\cup\Lambda)-d(p,Y\cup\Lambda))\,.

(28)

Indeed, if we let
D^{\prime}\subseteq D-D_{0}
be the subset of clients for which
d(p,c_{0})<d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda)
and
D^{\prime}_{0}\subseteq D_{0}
be the subset of clients for which
d(p,c_{0})<d(p,Y\cup\Lambda)
, then (
28
) equals

\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}}(d(p,c_{0})-d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}_{0}}(d(p,c_{0})-d(p,Y\cup\Lambda))

and we have

\eqref{eq:boringsubmodular1}\leq\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}}(d(p,c_{0})-d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}_{0}}(d(p,c_{0})-d(p,X\cup\Lambda))\,.

It follows that (
27
) is at most (
28
) because

\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}}(d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup Y\cup\Lambda)-d(p,\{\widetilde{\mu}(p)\}\cup X\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in D^{\prime}_{0}}(d(p,Y\cup\Lambda)-d(p,X\cup\Lambda))\leq 0\,.

To prove that
f
is submodular, it is thus sufficient to define
{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}
so that

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)\leq\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\,.

(29)

To this end, let
\Delta_{\text{new}}
contain the centers in
{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}-{\mathcal{R}}^{Y}_{1}
and those centers
c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}\cap\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
with
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})<\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)
. Notice that we may assume that, for each
c\in\Delta_{\text{new}}
,

\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})=\text{cost-inc}_{c}(\{c_{0}\})=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{if $C_{c}$ is hit by $c_{0}$,}\\
\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}}(d(p,c_{0})-d(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda))&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

because the addition of
c_{0}
caused a decrease in
\text{cost-inc}(\cdot)
for these clusters. Further, let
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
contain
\Delta_{\text{new}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
and the centers
\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}-\mathcal{R}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}
. So
\mathcal{R}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}=(\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y})\cup\Delta_{\text{new}}
and
|\Delta_{\text{new}}|=|\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}|
. We now define
\mathcal{R}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}=(\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X})\cup\Delta_{\text{new}}
where
\Delta^{X}_{\text{old}}
is obtained as follows:

•

Initialize
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}=\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}\cap\Delta_{\text{new}}
.

•

While
|\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}|<|\Delta_{\text{new}}|
, add a center according to the following priorities:

–

If there is a center
c\in\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
so that
c\not\in\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
, add
c
to
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
.

–

Else add a center
c\in\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
with
c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
.

We claim that the above is well-defined, i.e., that if
|\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}|<|\Delta_{\text{new}}|
and there is no center

c\in\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
so that
c\not\in\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
, then there must be a center
c\in\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
with
c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
. This is because
|\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}|<|\Delta_{\text{new}}|=|\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}|
and all remaining centers
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{X}-\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
are in
\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
in this case, so at least one of them must be in
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
(recall that
|\mathcal{R}_{1}^{X}|=|\mathcal{R}_{1}^{Y}|
).

With this notation we have

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{new}}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{new}}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\,.

(30)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{new}}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{new}}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)\,,

(31)

where we used that
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})\leq\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)
for any center
c
.

To analyze this, let
\pi
be a bijection from
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
to
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
so that
\pi(c)=c
for all
c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}\cap\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
.

Claim 9
.

For every
c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
we have

\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\leq\text{cost-inc}_{\pi(c)}(X)\,.

Proof.

If
c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}\cap\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
, the claim holds because
\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\leq\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)
for every
c\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
. Otherwise, we have
\pi(c)\not\in\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
. This is because
(\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}-\Delta_{\text{new}}^{Y})\cap\mathcal{R}_{1}^{Y}=\emptyset
. Indeed, in the construction of
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
, we initialized with
\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}\cap\Delta_{\text{new}}
and, as
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
contains
\Delta_{\text{new}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
, all elements in
\mathcal{R}^{X}_{1}\cap\Delta_{\text{new}}
are either in
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}
or not in
\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
. The property is then maintained by definition of the while-loop in the construction of
\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}
.

It follows that
\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
where we remove
c
and add
\pi(c)
is another subset of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of cardinality
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
. Hence, by the selection of
\mathcal{R}^{Y}_{1}
(to be the collection of centers with smallest
\text{cost-inc}(\cdot)
) we have

\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\leq\text{cost-inc}_{\pi(c)}(Y)\leq\text{cost-inc}_{\pi(c)}(X)\,.

∎

By the above claim,

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)-\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{X}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)=\sum_{c\in\Delta_{\text{old}}^{Y}}(\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)-\text{cost-inc}_{\pi(c)}(X))\leq 0

and thus by the above arguments (see (
30
) and (
31
))

\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{X}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(X)\leq\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y+c_{0}}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y\cup\{c_{0}\})-\sum_{c\in{\mathcal{R}}^{Y}_{1}}\text{cost-inc}_{c}(Y)\,.

We have thus verified (
29
), which concludes the proof that
f
is submodular.

Proof of Property 3.

For every
c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\text{closedcost}_{c}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=\min_{c^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{core}_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{c^{\prime}\in\Lambda}(\sum_{p\in C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime}))=\min_{c^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime}).

Thus

\displaystyle g(\emptyset)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\text{closedcost}_{c}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\min_{c^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}}\min_{c^{\prime}\in\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})\overset{\eqref{eq:MprimeDbound}}{\leq}\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{D},\mu^{\prime}_{D})\,.

Proof of Property 4.

Consider any
c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}
. If
C_{c}
is hit by
\hat{\text{OPT}}
one has

\displaystyle\text{closedcost}_{c}(\hat{\text{OPT}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda)\leq\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime}).

Otherwise

\displaystyle\text{closedcost}_{c}(\hat{\text{OPT}})

\displaystyle=\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{core}_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}(\sum_{p\in C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{core}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime}))=\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime}).

Hence in both cases
\text{closedcost}_{c}(\hat{\text{OPT}})\leq\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})
. Thus

\displaystyle g(\hat{\text{OPT}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\text{closedcost}_{c}(\hat{\text{OPT}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup\hat{\text{OPT}}\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}}\min_{c^{\prime}\in\hat{\text{OPT}}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})\overset{\eqref{eq:MprimeObound}}{\leq}\,\text{cost}(M^{\prime}_{O},\mu^{\prime}_{O}).

Proof of Property 5.

Let
X\subseteq F_{\mathcal{B}}
be independent in
M_{\mathcal{B}}
, i.e.,
X\in\mathcal{I}
. We give a polynomial-time algorithm that outputs
k
centers
S^{*}
whose cost is at most
(1+2\varepsilon)g(X)+15\varepsilon\text{opt}
. Let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
be a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of cardinality
|\mathcal{R}_{1}|
so that

\displaystyle g(X)=\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)

By the arguments in the proof of the first property, we can calculate
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
in polynomial time. Now we obtain
S^{*}
from
S_{\mathcal{Q}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}
as follows:

•

Initialize
S^{*}=S_{\mathcal{Q}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}}=S\cup\Lambda-\mathcal{Q}-\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
.

•

Remove centers
\mathcal{R}_{0}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
from
S^{*}
to obtain a set of cardinality
k
.

•

Finally, for each ball in
\mathcal{B}
, if
X
contains a center
c
in that ball, replace the associated dummy center by
c
; otherwise, replace the dummy center with an arbitrary center in the ball.

By the definition of the dummy centers
\Lambda
, the cost of
S^{*}
is at most

\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)\,.

To relate this to
g(X)
we need to compare
\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)
to
\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)
for every
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
.
Recall that

\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)=\begin{cases}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)&\mbox{if $C_{c}$ is hit by $X$,}\\
\min_{c^{\prime}\in X\cup\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{\text{core}}_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Consider a cluster
C_{c}
with
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
and partition
C_{c}-C^{\text{core}}_{c}
into sets
A
and
B
where
A
contain those clients
p
so that
d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)=d(p,c)
and
B
contain the remaining clients
p
with
d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)=d(p,X\cup\Lambda)
. Suppose first that there is a client
p_{0}\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}
so that
d(p_{0},X\cup\Lambda)<d(p_{0},c)
, i.e.,
C_{c}
is hit by
X\cup\Lambda
.
By the definition of
C^{\text{core}}_{c}
, we have
d(p_{0},c)\leq\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{cost}(S)}{|\mathcal{R}|\cdot|C_{c}|}\leq 5\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|\cdot|C_{c}|}
where we used that
\text{cost}(S)\leq 5\cdot\text{opt}
by Lemma
4.2
. So, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p\in C_{c}-B}(d(p,c)+d(c,p_{0})+d(p_{0},X\cup\Lambda))+\sum_{p\in B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p\in A}d(p,c)+\sum_{p\in B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+15\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\,,

where we used
d(p_{0},X\cup\Lambda)\leq d(c,p_{0})\leq 5\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|\cdot|C_{c}|}
and
d(p,c)\leq 5\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|\cdot|C_{c}|}
for any
p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}
for the last inequality. Moreover, by definition of
A
and
B
, we have
d(p,c)=d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)
for any
p\in A
and
d(p,X\cup\Lambda)=d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)
for any
p\in B
. Hence, in this case,

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)\leq\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{\text{core}}_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup Y)+15\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\leq\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)+15\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\,.

Let us now consider a cluster
C_{c}
with
c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}
that is not hit by
X\cup\Lambda
, particularly not by
X
.
Then

\displaystyle\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)

\displaystyle=\min_{c^{\prime}\in X\cup\Lambda}\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}}d(p,c^{\prime})+\sum_{p\in C_{c}-C^{\text{core}}_{c}}d(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}\cup B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{q\in A}d(q,c)

We further have

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}\cup B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{q\in A}d(q,X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}\cup B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{q\in A}\frac{1}{|C^{\text{core}}_{c}|}\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}}(d(q,c)+d(c,p)+d(p,X\cup\Lambda))

by the triangle inequality. Now using that
d(c,p)\leq 5\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|\cdot|C_{c}|}
we obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{|A|}{|C^{\text{core}}_{c}|}\right)\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}\cup B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{q\in A}d(q,c)+5\varepsilon\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon}{(1-\varepsilon)}\right)\sum_{p\in C^{\text{core}}_{c}\cup B}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{q\in A}d(q,c)+5\varepsilon\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)+5\varepsilon\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}

where for the second inequality we used that every cluster in
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
is concentrated. In more detail
|C^{\text{core}}_{c}|\geq(1-\varepsilon)|C_{c}|
, hence
|A|\leq|C_{c}-C^{\text{core}}_{c}|\leq\varepsilon|C_{c}|
.

Hence, for any
c\in\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\prime}
we proved that
\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)\leq(1+2\varepsilon)\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)+15\varepsilon\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}
. We thus get that the cost of
S^{*}
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\left((1+2\varepsilon)\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)+15\varepsilon\frac{\text{opt}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1+2\varepsilon)\left(\sum_{c\not\in\mathcal{R}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\sum_{p\in C_{c}}d\left(p,\{c\}\cup X\cup\Lambda\right)+\sum_{p\in D_{0}}d(p,X\cup\Lambda)+\sum_{c\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}}\text{closedcost}_{c}(X)\right)+15\varepsilon\text{opt}

which equals
(1+2\varepsilon)g(X)+15\varepsilon\text{opt}
.

4.7
Putting Everything Together
– Proof of Theorem
1.3

We now turn to proving Theorem
1.3
. We first describe the algorithm we analyse.

A
(2{+\varepsilon})
-Approximation for
k
-Median on an
\varepsilon/\log n
-
Stable Instance
k,D,F,d
.

1:

S\leftarrow localSearch()

\triangleright
Section
4.1

2:

W\leftarrow D
-
Sample(S,s^{*})

\triangleright
Section
4.2

3:

\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ball}}\leftarrow ballGuess(S,W)

\triangleright
Section
4.3

4:

for
all
\mathcal{B}\in\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ball}}

do

5:

Compute the dummy centers
\Lambda
according to
\mathcal{B}

6:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{exp}}\leftarrow epxRem(S,\Lambda)

\triangleright
Section
4.4

7:

for
all
\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{L}_{\text{exp}}
and all valid values of
|\mathcal{U}|
,
|\mathcal{R}|
and
|\mathcal{X}|

do

8:

\mathcal{L}_{cheap}\leftarrow\emptyset

9:

cheapRem(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)

\triangleright
Section
4.5

10:

for
all
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{L}_{cheap}
with the associated
\widetilde{\mu}

do

11:

Approximately solve the submodular maximization problem subject to a partition matroid induced by
(S,W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu})
and turn the obtained solution into a
k
-median solution
S_{S,W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu}}

\triangleright
Section
4.6

12:

return
the cheapest solution of type
S_{S,W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu}}

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Our approach consists of running the above algorithm

\log n
times and taking the minimum cost solution output.
In the remaining, we argue that the above algorithm yields a
2+O(\varepsilon)
-approximation with probability at least
(1-\varepsilon)(1-1/n)
. The claim then follows as the probability that all
\log n
executions of the above algorithm would fail is at most
\left(1-(1-\varepsilon)(1-1/n)\right)^{\log n}<1/n
, and so with high probability we output a
2+O(\varepsilon)
-approximation.

We first discuss the algorithm’s running time.
The local search algorithm runs in polynomial time.
The submodular optimization step also runs in polynomial time by Lemma
4.14
.
The output of
D
-
Sample()
(which is clearly a polynomial time procedure) is
a subset of size
s^{*}
. By Lemma
4.11
, we construct
\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ball}}
in polynomial time and
|\mathcal{L}_{\texttt{ball}}|\leq n^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
. Similarly, by Lemma
4.11
, we construct
\mathcal{L}_{\text{exp}}
in polynomial time and
|\mathcal{L}_{\text{exp}}|\leq n^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
. Finally, by Lemma
4.13
, we construct in polynomial-time

n^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
candidate pairs
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu}
. It follows that we solve
n^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
submodular function optimization problems, each in polynomial time, and thus the total running time is polynomial.

We turn to proving the approximation guarantee.
We aim to show that there exists a

(W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu})
that is successful with probability at least
(1-\varepsilon)(1-1/n)
.
Assuming this, the
(2+O(\varepsilon))
-approximation follows by combining
Claim
7
and Lemma
4.14
.

The local search step provides a 5 approximation
S
that satisfies Lemma
4.4
. Our algorithm next applies

D
-
Sample()
and by Lemma
4.5

finds a successful
W
with probability at least

1-\varepsilon
. Condition on having a successful

W
, we then have that Lemma
4.6
holds, and so does Lemma
4.7
by combining Lemma
4.4
and Lemma
4.6
.

Next, Lemma
4.8
implies that one
set of balls
\mathcal{B}
is a valid set of balls. This
set of balls induces a set of dummy centers

\Lambda
. From there, by Lemma
4.11
we have that
with probability at least
1-1/n
, the

expRem()
procedure produces a set
of center
\mathcal{Q}
such that

1.

\mathcal{Q}\subseteq S_{0}
, and

2.

The total cost in solution
S-Q\cup\Lambda
of the clusters in
S_{0}-\mathcal{Q}
is at most
\varepsilon\cdot\text{opt}
.

and so we have obtained a successful

(W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q})
and Lemma
4.12

applies. Condition on the event that we obtain
a successful
(W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q})
, then, applying Lemma
4.13
we have that
the
cheapRem()
procedure outputs a pair

(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu})
satisfying the properties of
Lemma
4.13
.
We thus have a
(W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu})
such that
W
is successful, the set of balls
\mathcal{B}
from Section
4.3
is valid,
\mathcal{Q}
selected in Section
4.4
satisfies the properties of Lemma
4.11
, and
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu}
selected in this section satisfies the properties of Lemma
4.13
and
so
(W,\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q},\widetilde{\mathcal{U}},\widetilde{\mu})
is successful. Finally, note that the only probabilistic steps were the sampling of
W
and of
\mathcal{Q}
, and both steps are successful with probability at least
(1-\varepsilon)\cdot(1-1/n)
, as required.
∎

Appendix A
Analysis of Robust
LogAdaptive

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.10
, showing that a solution
\mathcal{H}
of
\eta
-valid sequences yields an almost
2
-LMP approximation.
The proof is almost identical to that for
LogAdaptive
presented in Section
3.3
. There are only two minor changes:

1.

Definition
3
of
\eta
-openability allows a facility to be paid up to an
n\eta
-additive difference. This difference only appears in Lemma
A.1
below, which is an analog of Lemma
3.7
.

Lemma A.1
.

We have
\sum_{j\in D}\alpha^{*}_{j}\geq(1-\delta)\sum_{j\in D}d(j,S^{*})+\sum_{i\in S_{\mathsf{reg}}^{*}}(\hat{f}-n\eta)
.

2.

Definiton
4
of a
\eta
-valid sequence allows
h_{t}
is
\eta
-openable with respect to a super set
S^{\prime}\supseteq S\cup\{h_{1},\dots,h_{t-1}\}
. This difference only appears in (the last part of) the proof of Lemma
A.2
, an analog of Lemma
3.8
.

Lemma A.2
.

For a facility
i
,

\ \sum_{j\in D}[\alpha^{*}_{j}-2d(i,j)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

These two lemmas immediately imply Theorem
3.10
. The other supporting claims and lemmas (Claim
33
and Lemma
34
) remain identical.

In order to use mostly the same analysis, one syntactic difference between the setting of
Section
3.3
and the current setting that we have to reconcile is that the former analyzes the outcome of
LogAdaptive
while the latter analyzes a solution
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\dots,H_{L}\}
of
\eta
-valid sequences.
To use the same terminology from Section
3.3
, let us consider the
execution
of the solution
\mathcal{H}
where for
p=1,\dots,L
, we run the
p
-th phase according to the sequence
H_{p}
; in Stage 1, each facility in the sequence becomes open one by one with the corresponding
(\tau_{j})_{j}
values, and in Stage 2 at the end of the phase,
\theta\leftarrow\theta(1+\varepsilon^{2})
.

Then an
atomic step
can still be defined in the same way as in
LogAdaptive
; it corresponds to opening a facility in Stage 1 (and updating
\alpha,S,A
accordingly) or increasing
\alpha
values in Stage 2 simultaneously (and updating
S
accordingly). By “at any point of the algorithm”, we mean any point in the algorithm’s execution that is not in the middle of an atomic step.
The set of open facilities
S
can be partitioned to be the set of the open free facilities
S_{\mathsf{free}}
and the set of the open regular facilities
S_{\mathsf{reg}}
.

Throughout the analysis, we let
\alpha^{*}_{j}
be the final
\alpha
-value of a client
j\in D
, and we let
S^{*}
be the set of opened facilities. (Also let
S_{\mathsf{free}}^{*}
and
S_{\mathsf{reg}}^{*}
be the set of finally open free and regular facilities respectively.)
We start by analyzing the approximation guarantee of the algorithm with respect to
\alpha^{*}
, and we then prove that
\alpha^{*}/2
is a feasible solution to the dual.

A.1
Approximation Guarantee.

We prove Lemma
A.1
restated below. The only difference from Lemma
3.7

is that opening a facility only requires
\hat{f}-n\eta
instead of
\hat{f}
.
See
A.1

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

The proof is by induction on the algorithm that at any point of the algorithm

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I}\alpha_{j}\geq\sum_{j\in I}(1-\delta)d(j,S)+\sum_{i\in S_{\mathsf{reg}}}(\hat{f}-n\eta)\,.

(32)

The equality is initially true since
A=D
(thus
I=\emptyset
) and
S=\emptyset
. We now analyze each of the two stages separately.

Stage 1.

Consider what happens when we open a facility
h
, i.e., add it to
S
.

If
h
is a free facility, the
\alpha
values and
S_{\mathsf{reg}}
do not change, and the only change is some client
j
with
\alpha_{j}\geq(1-\delta)d(j,S)
becoming inactive. The amount of the increase of the left-hand side of (
32
) is at least that of the right-hand side.

If
h
is a regular facility
i
, let
(\alpha,S,A,\theta)
be the state right before opening
i
,
A^{\prime}
be the clients that are removed from
A
by the opening, which means
A^{\prime}=\{j\in A:\tau_{j}\geq(1-\delta)d(i,j)\}
. (Recall that the opening lets
\alpha_{j}\leftarrow\tau_{j}
for
j\in A
.)
Further, let
X:=\{j\in I:d(j,i)<d(j,S)\}
be the subset of clients of
I
that make positive bids to
i
.
The change of cost of the right-hand side of (
32
) is at most

(\hat{f}-n\eta)+\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}(1-\delta)d(i,j)+\sum_{j\in X}(1-\delta)(d(i,j)-d(j,S))\,.

Since
i
is paid up to
\eta
(the third bullet of Definition
3
), we also have

\displaystyle\hat{f}-n\eta

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}(\tau_{j}-(1-\delta)d(i,j))+\sum_{j\in X}(1-\delta)(d(j,S)-d(i,j))\,.

We thus get that the change of cost of the right-hand-side is at most
\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}}\tau_{j}
, which is the change of the left-hand-side.

Stage 2.

No facility is open at this stage, and every client
j
that becomes inactive at this stage has
\alpha_{j}=(1-\delta)d(j,S)
, which implies that the left-hand-side and right-hand-side both increase with the same amount when a client
j
becomes inactive.
∎

A.2
Dual Feasibility

We prove Lemma
A.2
for dual feasibility. The following claim is identical to
Claim
4
without any modification.

Claim 10

(No Over-Bidding)
.

At any point of the algorithm, for every facility
i
,

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(j,i)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(33)

Proof.

In the beginning, it is true because every client is active and
\alpha_{j}=1\leq d(i,j)
for every
j\in D
.
Let us prove that each step of the algorithm maintains this invariant. We first consider the steps that happen during the first stage and then those during the second stage.

Stage 1.

Suppose that facility
h^{\prime}
is open with the bids
(\tau_{j})_{j\in A}
.
It does (1) potentially increase the
\alpha
-values of clients in
B(h^{\prime},\varepsilon\theta)\cap A
, and (2) make clients in
A\cap B(h^{\prime},\theta/(1-\delta))
inactive, which includes
B(h^{\prime},\varepsilon\theta)\cap A
considered in (1).

Consider any facility
i
and see how (
33
) is impacted by this change.
First, observe that a client
j
that was inactive before the opening of
h^{\prime}
does not increase its contribution to (
33
). This is because
d(j,S)
monotonically decreases in
S
.
We now turn our attention to the more interesting case.
For a client
j
becoming inactive from active, the contribution to (
33
) changes from
[\alpha_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}
(where
\alpha
-values are measured right before the opening) to

[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(j,i)]^{+}
(where
S
includes
h^{\prime}
).

If
\alpha_{j}
does not change by the opening of
h^{\prime}
, the fact that
j
becomes inactive means
(1-\delta)d(j,S)\leq\alpha_{j}
, so
j
’s contribution to (
33
) cannot increase.
If
\alpha_{j}
does strictly increase by the opening of
h^{\prime}
, it means that
d(j,S)\leq d(j,h^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon\theta
after the opening, so the contribution to (
33
) decreases by at least
\theta/2
or becomes
0
.

We have thus proved that no client increases their contribution to (
33
) during the steps of the first stage, and so these steps maintain the inequality.

Stage 2.

Assume towards contradiction that at the end of the phase, if we increase
\alpha_{j}
of every
j\in A
to
\min((1+\varepsilon^{2})\theta,(1-\delta)d(j,S))
, the claim is violated for a non-empty subset of facilities. Since any free facility is only farther away in the metric (depending on
u(\cdot)
) we thus must have that the claim is violated by a set of regular facilities. Let
F^{\prime}
be that set.
We select a “minimal” such counter example in the following way: let
\tau^{\prime}\leq(1+\varepsilon^{2})\theta
be the smallest value so that
\alpha^{\prime}_{j}:=\min(\tau^{\prime},(1-\delta)d(j,S))
satisfies

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha_{j}^{\prime}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(j,i)]^{+}=\hat{f},

for some
i\in F^{\prime}
.
We remark that
\tau^{\prime}\geq\theta
since (
33
) is satisfied for all facilities after the first stage by the previous arguments.
Furthermore,
i\not\in S
is not yet open since if it was open then there would be no client
j\in A
for which
\alpha^{\prime}_{j}-d(i,j)
is strictly positive. Hence the increase in
\alpha
-values cannot cause
i
to violate (
33
) if it were already opened, which would contradict
i\in F^{\prime}
.

We now show that this
i
with
\tau_{j}=\alpha^{\prime}_{j}
for
j\in A\cap B(i,\varepsilon\theta)
and
\tau_{j}=\alpha_{j}
for
j\in A\setminus B(i,\varepsilon\theta)
satisfies the conditions of Definition
3
for
i
with respect to
S
. In other words,
i
is an
\eta
-openable facility with respect to
S
(that was not yet opened), which contradicts the
maximality
in Definition
4
for a
\eta
-valid sequence.

We verify the conditions of Definition
3
one-by-one. The first two bullets are satisfied by the definition of the bids
(\tau_{j})_{j\in A}
.
For the third bullet,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A}[\tau_{j}-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^{+}+(1-\delta)\sum_{j\in I}[d(j,S)-d(i,j)]^{+}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A\cap B(i,\varepsilon\theta)}[\alpha^{\prime}_{j}-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in A\setminus B(i,\varepsilon\theta)}[\alpha_{j}-(1-\delta)d(i,j)]^{+}+(1-\delta)\sum_{j\in I}[d(j,S)-d(i,j)]^{+}

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A\cap B(i,\varepsilon\theta)}[\alpha^{\prime}_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in A\setminus B(i,\varepsilon\theta)}[\alpha^{\prime}_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i,j)]^{+}\,.

For the second summation we used that
\alpha_{j}-(1-\delta)d(i,j)\geq(1+\varepsilon^{2})\alpha_{j}-d(i,j)\geq\alpha^{\prime}_{j}-d(i,j)
when
d(i,j)\geq\varepsilon\theta
(recall that
\alpha_{j}=\theta
for all
j\in A
).

It remains to verify the fourth bullet of Definition
3
. Suppose toward contradiction that there is a regular facility
i_{0}
and
k\in A
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A}[\tau_{j}-d(i_{0},j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[\tau_{k}-d(i_{0},k)-2d(i_{0},j)]^{+}>\hat{f}.

We would like to show

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A}[\tau_{j}-d(i_{0},j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i_{0},j)]^{+}

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in A}[\tau_{j}-d(i_{0},j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[\tau_{k}-d(i_{0},k)-2d(i_{0},j)]^{+}

which concludes the proof because then
i_{0}
is violated with a smaller value of
\tau^{\prime}
which contradicts the minimal selection of
\tau^{\prime}
.
To prove the above inequality, note that
k\in A
implies
\tau_{k}\leq(1-\delta)d(k,S)
and for every
j\in I
,

(1-\delta)d(k,S)\leq(1-\delta)(d(k,i_{0})+d(i_{0},j)+d(j,S)),

and so

\tau_{k}-d(i_{0},k)-2d(i_{0},j)\leq(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i_{0},j)\,.

∎

As usual, fix an arbitrary regular facility
i\in F
. The setup in this paragraph is also identical to Section
3.3
.
Let

D^{*}=\{j\in D:\alpha^{*}_{j}>2d(i,j)\}
be those clients that contribute to the left-hand-side. Further, order the clients in
D^{*}
according to
\alpha^{*}
values by the time they are removed from
A
(in the algorithm) and break ties according to
\alpha^{*}
values (in increasing order). We will use
j,k
to denote clients in
D^{*}
, and let us slightly abuse notation and use
j\leq k
with respect to this ordering. In other words, we have
j\leq k
if
j
was removed from
A
in an atomic step before
k
was removed,
j
and
k
was removed during the same atomic step and
\alpha^{*}_{j}\leq\alpha^{*}_{k}
.
We also use
the terminology that a facility
i

freezes

i_{0}
if
i
becomes open when
i_{0}
is not, and
d(i,i_{0})\leq(1+3\varepsilon)\theta/2
. (Of course,
i
freezes itself when it is open.)

The following lemma and the proof are also identical to Lemma
7
.

Lemma A.3
.

For any
k\in D^{*}
that becomes inactive strictly before
i
becomes frozen,

\sum_{j\in D^{*}:j\geq k}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in D^{*}:j<k}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(i,j)-d(i,k)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(34)

Proof.

Let us do the following case analysis depending on the stage
k
becomes inactive.

Stage 1.

k
becomes inactive because of the opening of some
h_{0}
. By the statement of the lemma, it suffices to handle the case that
h_{0}
does not freeze
i
. So
d(i,h_{0})>(1+3\varepsilon)\theta/2
. The opening of
h_{0}
might have strictly increased the
\alpha
-value of the clients in
B(h_{0},\varepsilon\theta)
, but no such client
j
will be in
D^{*}
, since they immediately become inactive while

d(i,j)\geq d(i,h_{0})-d(h_{0},j)>((1+3\varepsilon)/2-\varepsilon)\theta\geq(1+\varepsilon^{2})\theta/2\geq\alpha^{*}_{j}/2.

Applying this argument to every facility open in this phase (which did not freeze
i
), we can conclude that, right before
h_{0}
is open, every
j\in D^{*}
has
\alpha_{j}\leq\theta
and all active ones have
\alpha_{j}=\theta
. (So,
\alpha^{*}_{k}=\theta
as well since the opening of
h_{0}
does not strictly increase
\alpha_{k}
.)
Claim
33
, applied right before
h_{0}
is open, ensures that

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i,j)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

This satisfies (
34
) as (1) every
j\in D^{*}
with
j\geq k
is in
j\in A
at that point, and (2) for every
j<k
with
j\in I
,

\alpha^{*}_{k}<(1-\delta)d(k,S)\leq(1-\delta)d(j,S)+d(j,i)+d(i,k)

implies

\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(i,j)-d(i,k)\leq(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i,j).

(Note that this
S
does not contain
h_{0}
and
k
was not connected yet.)

Stage 2.

Suppose that
k
becomes inactive by the increase of the
\alpha
-values at the end of a phase.
Then Claim
33
ensures that, at the end of this phase (after
k
becomes inactive),

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(j,i)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(35)

Let us see that the left-hand-side of (
35
) is at least that of (
34
) by comparing the contribution of
j\in D^{*}
to both. If
j\in A
, its contribution to the former is definitely at least that to the latter, since any active
j
has
\alpha_{j}=(1+\varepsilon^{2})\theta\geq\alpha^{*}_{k}
.

For
j\in I
, we must have

\alpha^{*}_{k}\leq(1-\delta)d(k,S)\leq(1-\delta)(d(k,j)+d(j,S))\,.

(since
\alpha_{k}
is never increased above
(1-\delta)d(k,S)
).
and so by rearranging and using the triangle inequality
(d(k,j)\leq d(k,i)+d(i,j)
) we get

\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(i,j)-d(i,k)\leq(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(i,j)

which satisfies our goal when
j<k
. Finally, when
j\in I
but
j\geq k
, it means that
j
was removed at the same time as
k
at the end of the phase. In that case
(1-\delta)d(j,S)=\alpha^{*}_{j}
and, as we break ties with
\alpha^{*}
-values,
\alpha^{*}_{j}\geq\alpha^{*}_{k}
, which again satisfies our goal.
∎

Equipped with the above lemma, we are ready to complete the proof of dual feasibility.

Proof of Lemma
A.2
.

Consider the case that
i
is frozen by
h_{0}
with
(\tau_{j})_{j\in A}
.
The following claim also remains unchanged from Section
3.3
.

Claim
.

We have
\alpha^{*}_{j}=\tau_{j}
for every
j\in D^{*}\cap A
.

Proof of Claim.

Consider
j\in A
.
First, note that if
\tau_{j}\geq(1-\delta)d(j,h_{0})
then
j
is removed from
A
when
h_{0}
is opened and so
\alpha^{*}_{j}=\tau_{j}
.

In the other case, when
\alpha_{j}\leq\tau_{j}<(1-\delta)d(j,h_{0})
we show that
j\not\in D^{*}
.
Consider the (future) time right after
j
is removed from the active clients. The
\alpha
-value of
j
then (which is equal to the final
\alpha^{*}_{j}
) cannot be strictly greater than
(1-\delta)d(j,h_{0})
; whether it is increased in Stage 1 (as
\alpha\leftarrow\tau
) or Stage 2, since
h_{0}\in S
(where
S
is the set of open facilities right before
j
becomes inactive), the algorithm ensures that it cannot be strictly more than
(1-\delta)d(j,S)\leq(1-\delta)d(j,h_{0})
.
By the triangle inequality

\displaystyle d(j,i)\geq d(j,h_{0})-d(h_{0},i)=\left(1-\frac{d(h_{0},i)}{\theta}\frac{\theta}{d(j,h_{0})}\right)d(j,h_{0})\,,

where
\theta
is the value when
h_{0}
was open. As
h_{0}
freezes
i
, we have
d(h_{0},i)\leq(1+3\varepsilon)\theta/2
and, by the assumption of the case we have
\theta=\alpha_{j}\leq(1-\delta)d(j,h_{0})
. Plugging in those bounds to the above inequality yields,

\displaystyle d(j,i)\geq\left(1-\frac{(1+3\varepsilon)}{2}(1-\delta)\right)d(j,h_{0})\,.

We rewrite the above inequality (multiplying both sides by two and using that
\delta=3\varepsilon
) to obtain

\displaystyle 2d(j,i)\geq\left(2-{(1+3\varepsilon)}(1-\delta)\right)d(j,h_{0})=\left(1+9\varepsilon^{2}\right)d(j,h_{0})\,.

In other words we have
\alpha^{*}_{j}\leq d(j,h_{0})<2d(j,i)
, so
j
cannot be in
D^{*}
.
∎

The following remaining part of the proof needs some changes from Section
3.3
, since when
h_{0}
is
\eta
-openable in the
\eta
-openable sequence, it might be with respect to a superset
S^{\prime}\supseteq S
. (Here
S
denotes the set of all opened facilities right before
h_{0}
.)
Let
A^{\prime}
the subset of
A
that is still active with respected to the superset
S^{\prime}
for opening
h_{0}
and let
I^{\prime}=A\setminus A^{\prime}
.
As the
\alpha
-values of clients in
A\cap D^{*}
remain unchanged after the opening of
h_{0}
, the fourth bullet of Definition
3
ensures that for any
k\in A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
,

\sum_{j\in A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}}[\alpha^{*}_{j}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in(I\cup I^{\prime})\cap D^{*}}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(i,j)-d(k,i)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(36)

Moreover, for every client
k\in D^{*}\cap I
that became inactive strictly before
h_{0}
was opened, Lemma
34
says

\sum_{j\in D^{*}:j\geq k}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in D^{*}:j<k}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(i,j)-d(i,k)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(37)

Finally for a client in
k\in D^{*}\cap I^{\prime}
, note that
\alpha^{*}_{k}=\alpha_{k}=\theta
, because
k
was not active with respect to
S^{\prime}
. Therefore, by Claim
33
applied right before
h_{0}
was opened

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[(1-\delta)d(j,S)-d(j,i)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

which by the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
3.8
,

\sum_{j\in A}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-d(i,j)]^{+}+\sum_{j\in I}[\alpha^{*}_{k}-2d(j,i)-d(i,k)]^{+}\leq\hat{f}.

(38)

Let us consider the summation of (
37
) for every
k\in I\cap D^{*}
, (
38
) for every
k\in I^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
,
and (
36
) for every
k\in A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
,
and consider how many times each term appears.
Let
a^{\prime}=|A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}|,i^{\prime}=|I^{\prime}\cap D^{*}|,d=|D^{*}|
.

•

-d(i,j^{\prime})
when
j^{\prime}\in I\cap D^{*}
: Say
j^{\prime}
is the
\ell
th client in
D^{*}
for some
\ell\leq d-i^{\prime}-a^{\prime}
. Then
-d(i,j^{\prime})
appears
d+(d-\ell)
times in (
36
),
(
37
), and (
38
) as
-d(i,j)
, and
(\ell-1)
times as
-d(i,k)
in
(
37
) when
k=j^{\prime}
, so the total is
2d-1
.

•

-d(i,j^{\prime})
when
j^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
:
It appears
d+a^{\prime}
times in (
36
),
(
37
), and (
38
) as
-d(i,j)
, and
(d-i^{\prime}-a^{\prime})
times as
-d(i,k)
in
(
38
) when
k=j^{\prime}
, so the total is
2d-i^{\prime}
.

•

-d(i,j^{\prime})
when
j^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
:
It appears
d
times in (
36
),
(
37
), and (
38
) as
-d(i,j)
, and
(d-a^{\prime})
times as
-d(i,k)
in
(
36
) when
k=j^{\prime}
, so the total is
2d-a^{\prime}
.

•

\alpha^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\in I\cap D^{*}
:
d
times in (
37
) as
\alpha^{*}_{k}
when
k=j^{\prime}
.

•

\alpha^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
:
d
times in (
38
) as
\alpha^{*}_{k}
when
k=j^{\prime}
.

•

\alpha^{*}_{j^{\prime}}
for
j^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\cap D^{*}
:
(d-a^{\prime})
times in (
36
) as
\alpha^{*}_{k}
when
k=j^{\prime}
and once as
\alpha^{*}_{j}
in (
36
) for each
k\in A^{\prime}
, so it is still
d
times.

Therefore,
\sum_{j\in D^{*}}\left(\alpha^{*}_{j}-2d(i,j)\right)\leq\hat{f}
, which proves the lemma in the case the opening of a facility
h_{0}
freezes
i
.
To complete the proof, we note that the remaining case, when no facility freezes
i
, corresponds to the easier situation in the above analysis when
D^{*}\cap A=\emptyset
, i.e., it corresponds to summing up (
37
) over all clients in
D^{*}
, and so
\sum_{j\in D^{*}}\left(\alpha^{*}_{j}-2d(i,j)\right)\leq\hat{f}
also in that case.
∎
